[Entrance](<https://ciphermuseum.com/index.html>)› [Museum Map](<https://ciphermuseum.com/museum-map.html>)› Hall IX

 Hall IX · 1882 – Present

### The Unbreakable

Where classical cryptography reached its limits — and mathematics drew the line

Many historical ciphers succumbed to cryptanalysis; others were exposed by operational mistakes, became obsolete, or remain unresolved. Claude Shannon proved perfect secrecy for a correctly used one-time pad: a secret, uniformly random, independent message-length key used once. Its demanding key-distribution requirements limit its use. This hall examines that guarantee and how pad reuse undermined it.

What came next — AES, RSA, Diffie-Hellman, and the systems that protect the digital world — lives in the [Modern Cryptography](<https://ciphermuseum.com/modern.html>) wing.

Theoretical Security WWII

[Exhibit 36 1882 · Theoretical

One-Time Pad

A one-time pad offers perfect secrecy with a secret, uniform, independent message-length key used once. Try ciphertext tampering to see why secrecy is not authentication.

Plain: HELLO
 Key: XMCKL (illustrative pad)
 Cipher: EQNVZ

Secure (Modern) If Rules Followed

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/one-time-pad.html>) [Exhibit 37 1917 · AT&T

Vernam Cipher

Vernam’s teleprinter system combines text with a key stream. XOR supplies perfect secrecy only when the pad is uniformly random, independent, secret, long enough and never reused.

Plain: H (01000; illustrative A=1 encoding)
 Key: Q (10001)
 XOR: Y (11001)

Secure (Modern) XOR Foundation

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/vernam.html>) [Exhibit 108 1943–1980 · Cold War

VENONA Project

VENONA analysed intercepted Soviet messages, chiefly from the 1940s, by exploiting duplicated pad material. The project ran from 1943 to 1980.

Depth relation: Decimal: C₁ − C₂ = P₁ − P₂ (mod 10, digitwise)
 Failure mode: Key page reuse
 Lesson: Math stayed sound; operations failed

Broken Operational failure

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/venona.html>) [Exhibit 51 1999 · Schneier

Solitaire / Pontifex

Bruce Schneier’s 54-card Solitaire cipher (1999) was designed for Neal Stephenson’s novel *Cryptonomicon* (1999 edition). Crowley measured a bias in consecutive keystream outputs; this is a study exhibit, not a security recommendation.

Key: deck order · Output: 1–26 per card draw
 Bias: 1/22.5 vs 1/26 ideal (Crowley 1999)

Historical Only Statistical Weakness Cryptonomicon

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/solitaire.html>)

The Mathematics of Secrecy

#### Claude Shannon and Perfect Secrecy

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="../images/icons.svg#i-chart"></use></svg>
```

Shannon's 1949 Proof

Perfect secrecy means observing ciphertext does not change the prior probability of any plaintext: P(M=m | C=c) = P(M=m), for ciphertexts with nonzero probability. Plaintexts need not be equally likely beforehand. An independently uniform one-time pad meets this definition; it is not the only possible perfectly secret construction. Encryption alone does not authenticate a message.

For example, if a message was more likely before interception, it remains more likely afterwards. The ciphertext does not improve that prior guess. This guarantee concerns message content within the defined message space; length and other traffic metadata may still be visible.

Source: Claude E. Shannon, *Communication Theory of Secrecy Systems* (1949), §10, pp. 679–682. Shannon also describes perfectly secret systems using Latin squares.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="../images/icons.svg#i-diamond"></use></svg>
```

Why Its Use Is Limited

A one-time pad has demanding operational requirements:

- The key must be as long as the message — a 1GB file needs a 1GB key

- The key must be truly random — not pseudorandom

- Key distribution must be perfectly secure — if the key channel isn't secure, neither is the cipher

- Every key used once means you can never reuse, recycle, or re-derive it

⚡

**VENONA:** US Army analysts, and later successor organizations including NSA, exploited reused Soviet additive key material. The historical traffic used decimal subtraction without borrowing, not binary XOR; the exhibit shows both the principle and a decimal teaching exercise.

Why Classical Ciphers Failed

#### Shannon's Two Principles — and How Modern Crypto Achieves Them

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="../images/icons.svg#i-puzzle"></use></svg>
```

 Confusion

Confusion complicates the relationship between the key and ciphertext statistics. For a Caesar shift, C = P + k mod 26, one aligned plaintext–ciphertext letter pair reveals k. Shannon’s term is a design principle, not a universal numerical score.

**Modern solution:** AES uses a fixed, public nonlinear S-box together with key addition and diffusion over multiple rounds. The S-box alone is a known byte substitution; its output determines its input. Security belongs to the complete construction, not secrecy of that table.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="../images/icons.svg#i-globe"></use></svg>
```

 Diffusion

Spreading the influence of each plaintext bit across many ciphertext bits. *Caesar does not spread changes between letters* — changing one letter changes exactly one ciphertext letter. Frequency analysis exploits this directly.

**Modern comparison:** AES combines nonlinear substitution, key mixing and linear diffusion. A full-cipher avalanche measurement describes behavior across inputs, not a promise that every change flips exactly half the bits.

The Complete Journey

#### From Caesar to AES — What Each Generation Taught the Next

|  Classical Cipher Type |  Fatal Weakness |  Modern Solution |  Modern Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Caesar / Substitution | Frequency analysis — letter mapping preserved | Repeated substitution, diffusion and key mixing | AES SubBytes |
| Monoalphabetic | 26! keys but relabel the same frequency counts | Fixed public S-box combined with round-key addition | AES with round keys |
| Polyalphabetic / Vigenère | Repeating key creates detectable periodicity | Pseudorandom keystreams with unique nonce/counter inputs under a key; output bytes can repeat | ChaCha20, AES-CTR |
| Transposition | Letters preserved, just rearranged — anagram attacks work | Substitution and linear diffusion across rounds | AES ShiftRows + MixColumns |
| Playfair / Hill (block) | Small blocks leak digraph statistics; linear algebra solvable | 128-bit blocks, non-linear operations, key mixing | AES (128-bit block) |
| Military layered (ADFGVX) | Substitution + transposition — each layer still attackable | AES uses 10, 12 or 14 rounds; the final round omits MixColumns. Inversion with the key is efficient | AES |
| One-Time Pad | Impractical key management — reuse is catastrophic | Computational security with short keys under stated assumptions; not perfect secrecy | AES modes; authenticated X25519-based protocols |

🎓

**The museum’s lesson:** State the security goal and assumptions explicitly. Perfect secrecy, computational security, authenticity and implementation resistance are different claims. Real systems need appropriate parameters, secure key handling and careful implementation; they do not invariably fail loudly when misused.

[Entrance](<https://ciphermuseum.com/index.html>)› [Museum Map](<https://ciphermuseum.com/museum-map.html>)› Hall XII

 Hall XII · Open Problems · Living Research

### Unsolved Ciphers

Ten cases involving unresolved inscriptions, incomplete decipherments or uncertain interpretation. Their evidence and solution status differ; consult each exhibit’s dated sources.

  These cases have different evidential limits. The Voynich parchment’s radiocarbon range is not the date its text was written. Kryptos K4’s archival plaintext discovery is distinct from a public cryptanalytic solution. Some other inscriptions are not established encryption systems at all.

🧭

**Unsolved is not the same as unbreakable.** Some cases may be ciphers, others unknown writing, private notation or hoaxes. Archival plaintext discovery, a proposed interpretation and a reproducible cryptanalytic solution are distinct outcomes.

[XII · 1 Voynich Manuscript The Voynich manuscript has an undeciphered script. Parchment dating and sample-dependent text statistics constrain hypotheses without identifying its language or method. Parchment dated 1404–1438](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/voynich.html>) [XII · 2 Kryptos (K4) Kryptos: K1–K3 publicly solved; K4 plaintext recovered from an archive in 2025, without a publicly demonstrated cryptanalytic solution. 1990](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/kryptos.html>) [XII · 3 Beale Ciphers (1 & 3) Beale ciphers — three numerical ciphers published in an 1885 Lynchburg pamphlet, said to have been written in the 1820s and to point to a Virginia treasure. Only #2 was solved (Declaration of Independence key); #1 and #3 unsolved. Published 1885; claimed 1820s story](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/beale.html>) [XII · 4 Dorabella Cipher Edward Elgar’s 1897 Dorabella cryptogram contains 87 symbols. No proposed reading has achieved general acceptance. 1897](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/dorabella.html>) [XII · 5 Zodiac Z-13 & Z-32 The short Z13 and Z32 messages lack generally accepted readings. Their ambiguity differs from the solved Z408 and Z340 messages. 1970](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/zodiac.html>) [XII · 6 Shugborough Inscription An eighteenth-century garden monument with eight unexplained letters and D · M below. Its encoding method, if any, is unknown. Mid-18th century](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/shugborough.html>) [XII · 7 D'Agapeyeff Challenge Alexander d’Agapeyeff’s 1939 numeric challenge: a verified ciphertext excerpt, overlapping-pair analysis and explicit limits on proposed reconstructions. 1939](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/dagapeyeff.html>) [XII · 8 Somerton Man (Tamám Shud) The Somerton Man inscription and a simplified transcription experiment. A 2022 genealogical identification proposal is separate from interpreting the letters. 1948](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/somerton-man.html>) [XII · 9 McCormick Notes Two handwritten notes found with Ricky McCormick in 1999. The FBI’s 2011 appeal sought contextual evidence and help interpreting their notation. ~1999](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/mccormick.html>) [XII · 10 Phaistos Disc The Phaistos Disc is a Bronze Age stamped inscription from Crete. Its language and reading remain disputed; it is not established as a cipher. ~1700 BCE](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/phaistos-disc.html>)

📐

**How an "unsolved" exhibit can become a "solved" one.** The path runs through one or more of: (a) *statistical attack* — the Oranchak–Blake–Van Eycke solution of Z-340 in 2020; (b) *transcription and manual analysis aided by statistics* — Knight, Megyesi and Schaefer’s 2011 Copiale decipherment; (c) *archival discovery* — a key, plaintext, or contemporary commentary surfaces; (d) *cross-disciplinary linguistics* — Champollion's 1822 Egyptian decipherment is the model. Which approach is useful depends on the object and the evidence available.

[← Previous Hall XI: Modern Cryptography](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/modern-crypto.html>) [Next → Hall XIII: Cipher Culture](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/culture.html>)

#### The Story of Encryption

[##### Act I · Ancient World to Paper Systems

 c. 1900 BC – 1900

From unusual writing in Khnumhotep II’s tomb—not evidence of secret communication—to classical substitution, transposition and repeating-key systems.

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/ancient.html>) [##### Act II · Machines & War

 1900 – 1945

From WWI field systems to Enigma and Lorenz in WWII, ending with the first codebreaking computers.

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/machines.html>) [##### Act III · Mathematical Security

 1945 – Present

From post-war information theory to modern cryptography: Shannon's 1949 proof and the rise of mathematical security.

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/unbreakable.html>) [##### Annex · Hall of Foundations

 The Mathematics Under It All

 →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/index.html>)

```javascript
// Intersection observer for fade-up animations (graceful fallback for older browsers)
(function(){
  var els = document.querySelectorAll('.fade-up');
  if (typeof IntersectionObserver === 'undefined') {
    els.forEach(function(el){ el.style.animationPlayState = 'running'; });
    return;
  }
  var io = new IntersectionObserver(function(entries) {
    entries.forEach(function(e) { if (e.isIntersecting) { e.target.style.animationPlayState='running'; io.unobserve(e.target); }});
  }, {threshold:.1});
  els.forEach(function(el) { el.style.animationPlayState='paused'; io.observe(el); });
})();
```

#### Embedded script 3 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

'use strict';
(function(){
  var A = 'ABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ';
  var mod = function(a,m){ return((a%m)+m)%m; };

  var engines = {
    caesar: {
      enc: function(t){ return t.split('').map(function(ch){
        var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
        if(i<0) return ch; var o=A[mod(i+3,26)];
        return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
      }).join(''); },
      dec: function(t){ return t.split('').map(function(ch){
        var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
        if(i<0) return ch; var o=A[mod(i-3,26)];
        return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
      }).join(''); }
    },
    rot13: {
      enc: function(t){ return t.split('').map(function(ch){
        var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
        if(i<0) return ch; var o=A[mod(i+13,26)];
        return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
      }).join(''); },
      dec: function(t){ return this.enc(t); }
    },
    atbash: {
      enc: function(t){ return t.split('').map(function(ch){
        var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
        if(i<0) return ch; var o=A[25-i];
        return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
      }).join(''); },
      dec: function(t){ return this.enc(t); }
    },
    vigenere: {
      enc: function(t,k){
        k=(k||'MUSEUM').toUpperCase().replace(/[^A-Z]/g,'');
        if(!k) k='MUSEUM'; var ki=0;
        return t.split('').map(function(ch){
          var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
          if(i<0) return ch;
          var o=A[mod(i+A.indexOf(k[ki%k.length]),26)]; ki++;
          return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
        }).join('');
      },
      dec: function(t,k){
        k=(k||'MUSEUM').toUpperCase().replace(/[^A-Z]/g,'');
        if(!k) k='MUSEUM'; var ki=0;
        return t.split('').map(function(ch){
          var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
          if(i<0) return ch;
          var o=A[mod(i-A.indexOf(k[ki%k.length]),26)]; ki++;
          return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
        }).join('');
      }
    },
    beaufort: {
      enc: function(t,k){
        k=(k||'MUSEUM').toUpperCase().replace(/[^A-Z]/g,'');
        if(!k) k='MUSEUM'; var ki=0;
        return t.split('').map(function(ch){
          var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
          if(i<0) return ch;
          var o=A[mod(A.indexOf(k[ki%k.length])-i,26)]; ki++;
          return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
        }).join('');
      },
      dec: function(t,k){ return this.enc(t,k); }
    },
    affine: {
      enc: function(t){
        return t.split('').map(function(ch){
          var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
          if(i<0) return ch; var o=A[mod(5*i+8,26)];
          return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
        }).join('');
      },
      dec: function(t){
        return t.split('').map(function(ch){
          var c=ch.toUpperCase(); var i=A.indexOf(c);
          if(i<0) return ch; var o=A[mod(21*(i-8),26)];
          return ch===c?o:o.toLowerCase();
        }).join('');
      }
    },
    railfence: {
      enc: function(t){
        var rails=['','',''];
        var r=0,dir=1;
        for(var i=0;i<t.length;i++){
          rails[r]+=t[i];
          if(r===0) dir=1; if(r===2) dir=-1;
          r+=dir;
        }
        return rails.join('');
      },
      dec: function(t){
        var n=t.length,lens=[0,0,0],r=0,dir=1;
        for(var i=0;i<n;i++){ lens[r]++; if(r===0)dir=1;if(r===2)dir=-1;r+=dir; }
        var rows=['','',''],pos=0;
        for(var j=0;j<3;j++){ rows[j]=t.substr(pos,lens[j]); pos+=lens[j]; }
        var idx=[0,0,0]; r=0;dir=1; var out='';
        for(var i=0;i<n;i++){ out+=rows[r][idx[r]++]; if(r===0)dir=1;if(r===2)dir=-1;r+=dir; }
        return out;
      }
    },
    polybius: {
      enc: function(t){
        var grid='ABCDEFGHIKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ';
        return t.split('').map(function(ch){
          var c=ch.toUpperCase(); if(c==='J') c='I';
          var idx=grid.indexOf(c);
          if(idx<0) return ch;
          return ''+(Math.floor(idx/5)+1)+(idx%5+1);
        }).join(' ');
      },
      dec: function(t){
        var grid='ABCDEFGHIKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ';
        return t.replace(/([1-5])([1-5])/g,function(_,r,c){
          var idx=(parseInt(r)-1)*5+(parseInt(c)-1);
          return (idx>=0&&idx<25)?grid[idx]:'';
        });
      }
    },
    bacon: {
      // 25-letter Bacon (I/J merged). 25 × 5 = 125 chars.
      // A=AAAAA B=AAAAB C=AAABA D=AAABB E=AABAA F=AABAB G=AABBA H=AABBB
      // I/J=ABAAA K=ABAAB L=ABABA M=ABABB N=ABBAA O=ABBAB P=ABBBA Q=ABBBB
      // R=BAAAA S=BAAAB T=BAABA U=BAABB V=BABAA W=BABAB X=BABBA Y=BABBB Z=BBAAA
      enc: function(t){
        var codes='AAAAAAAAABAAABAAAABBAABAAAABABAABBAAABBBABAAAABAABABABAABABBABBAAABBABABBBAABBBBBAAAABAAABBAABABAABBBABAABABABBABBABABBBBBAAA';
        return t.split('').map(function(ch){
          var c=ch.toUpperCase(); if(c==='J') c='I'; var i=A.indexOf(c);
          if(i<0) return ch; if(i>9) i--;
          return codes.substr(i*5,5);
        }).join(' ');
      },
      dec: function(t){
        var codes='AAAAAAAAABAAABAAAABBAABAAAABABAABBAAABBBABAAAABAABABABAABABBABBAAABBABABBBAABBBBBAAAABAAABBAABABAABBBABAABABABBABBABABBBBBAAA';
        var clean=t.replace(/[^AaBb]/g,'').toUpperCase();
        var out='';
        for(var i=0;i+5<=clean.length;i+=5){
          var chunk=clean.substr(i,5);
          for(var j=0;j<25;j++){ if(codes.substr(j*5,5)===chunk){ var c=j; if(c>=9) c++; out+=A[c]; break; }}
        }
        return out;
      }
    }
  };

  var selCipher = document.getElementById('pg-cipher');
  var elInput   = document.getElementById('pg-input');
  var elOutput  = document.getElementById('pg-output');
  var elKey     = document.getElementById('pg-key');
  var elKeyWrap = document.getElementById('pg-key-wrap');
  var btnSwap   = document.getElementById('pg-swap');
  var btnCopy   = document.getElementById('pg-copy');
  var encrypting = true;

  function update(){
    var name = selCipher.value;
    var eng  = engines[name];
    var text = elInput.value;
    var key  = elKey.value;
    elKeyWrap.style.display = (name==='vigenere'||name==='beaufort')?'':'none';
    if(!text){ elOutput.textContent=''; return; }
    elOutput.textContent = encrypting ? eng.enc(text,key) : eng.dec(text,key);
  }

  selCipher.addEventListener('change', update);
  elInput.addEventListener('input', update);
  elKey.addEventListener('input', update);
  btnSwap.addEventListener('click', function(){
    encrypting = !encrypting;
    btnSwap.textContent = encrypting ? '↕ Decrypt' : '↕ Encrypt';
    var lbl = elInput.previousElementSibling;
    lbl.textContent = encrypting ? 'Plaintext' : 'Ciphertext';
    update();
  });
  btnCopy.addEventListener('click', function(){
    var text = elOutput.textContent;
    if(text && navigator.clipboard){
      navigator.clipboard.writeText(text);
      btnCopy.textContent = 'COPIED';
      setTimeout(function(){ btnCopy.textContent='COPY'; }, 1200);
    }
  });

  // Seed with a demo message
  elInput.value = 'The die is cast';
  update();
})();
```

Research and evidence

### How a Decipherment Becomes Evidence

Compare Copiale, Mary Stuart and Z340 with the Egyptian, Linear B and Maya decipherments: what structure, keys and independent checks can each establish.

A decipherment is a chain of claims: source → transcription → structural hypothesis → key → reading → independent checks. These investigations distinguish historical evidence from museum exercises. [Open the Evidence Workbench](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html>).

#### Copiale: the ordinary letters were the surprise

[The Copiale manuscript](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/copiale.html>) combines familiar Roman letters with unfamiliar symbols. Knight, Megyesi and Schaefer first tested the familiar letters as the message-bearing alphabet. That hypothesis failed. Comparing neighboring contexts helped reveal groups of homophones among the unfamiliar signs; German language hypotheses and human interpretation guided the search.

- **Source:** preserve diacritics and distinguish symbol types before counting them. A familiar-looking letter need not play its familiar role.

- **Competing hypothesis:** treat ordinary Roman letters as ciphertext, then as separators. The successful reading uses unaccented Roman letters as word boundaries. Calling them meaningless nulls loses that function.

- **Mixed key:** the system includes multi-letter values such as CH, SCH and ST, logograms, and a colon that doubles the preceding consonant. A one-letter substitution cannot represent the whole manuscript.

- **Evidence:** test mappings across occurrences and read the resulting German in context. Some material remained unresolved in the 2011 paper; a successful passage does not certify every glyph.

**Try it:** in the workbench, enter an original exercise `g1 g2 g3 g1` with g1→A, g2→CH and g3→space. Compare that typed key with forcing every sign to one letter. This exercise is invented; it is not a transcription from Copiale.

Source: [Knight, Megyesi and Schaefer, “The Copiale Cipher” (2011)](<https://aclanthology.org/W11-1202/>), especially the transcription and decipherment discussion, PDF pp. 2–7. The paper and its manuscript figures supply the historical evidence.

#### Mary Stuart: preserve the dot, then test the story

The [57-letter collection published in 2023](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/mary-stuart-castelnau-letters.html>) contains more than 150,000 transcribed cipher symbols and about 50,000 plaintext words. Its 219 graphical symbol types include distinctions that cannot be compressed into a 26-letter alphabet.

- **Source:** record the archive, shelfmark and folio. The collection spans BnF Français 2988, Français 20506, Cinq Cent de Colbert 470 and Français 3158. Keep letter identity separate from a modern filename.

- **Reading alternatives:** diacritics distinguish symbols. If a mark might be a dot or damage, record both readings and its image region. Do not silently merge the two types to make a solver work.

- **Language hypothesis:** early Italian expectations gave way to French. A modern-English score is not evidence against a French reading.

- **Search and interpretation:** simulated annealing recovered letter homophones. Names, word codes, repeat and erase controls, and other nomenclature required further manual work. Preserve the literal reading before normalizing spelling or translating.

- **Independent corroboration:** surviving plaintext versions and historical correspondence help test the interpretation. They provide evidence beyond the language score used to select a candidate.

**Try it:** transcribe an invented pair as `g17 g17-dot`, save a span with both alternatives, and record why one is preferred. Revise the source and inspect the earlier annotation: its original location is retained. This tests provenance, not recovery of a historical letter.

Source: [Lasry, Biermann and Tomokiyo, “Deciphering Mary Stuart’s lost letters from 1578–1584” (2023)](<https://doi.org/10.1080/01611194.2022.2160677>), §3.2, §4.2, §5.1 and Appendix A; PDF pp. 9–13, 16–26, 32 and 96–98. Figure 2 shows letter F38 and the importance of the source image.

#### Egyptian hieroglyphs: sound, sense and direction

**Egyptian hieroglyphs are a writing system, not a cipher.** Classical and Renaissance writers stressed the signs as symbols of ideas (Parkinson). That assumption, not encryption, was the obstacle: many of the signs recorded sounds. Scribes could still write unusually on purpose; see the [Khnumhotep II spellings](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/egyptian-substitution.html>).

- **Source.** The [Rosetta Stone](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/rosetta-stone.html>) records one decree in two languages and three scripts, and its Greek could be read. In the hieroglyphic text, royal names are enclosed in cartouches (Parkinson).

- **Transcription.** By 1816 Thomas Young had isolated Ptolemy’s name in a cartouche and, helped by the demotic version, gave five of its signs correct sound values and eight others wrong ones (Parkinson). Copies of further cartouches, from an obelisk found at Philae and from the temple of Abu Simbel, widened the comparison (Parkinson).

- **Structural hypothesis.** Young proposed that sound signs were used only for foreign names such as Ptolemy, and that the rest of the script, including the epithets inside the cartouche, worked symbolically (Parkinson). In 1821 Champollion still treated hieroglyphs as symbolic and logographic, one picture to a word (Parkinson).

- **Key.** The key that worked is a mixed system. Some signs write one, two or three consonants; some write a whole word by sound and meaning together; determinatives add no sound but place a word in a class, such as motion; and phonetic complements repeat part of a larger sign’s sound to make its reading clear (Collier and Manley; Parkinson). Vowels are not written (Collier and Manley).

- **Reading.** Signs face the beginning of the line, so you read into their faces, and from top to bottom (Collier and Manley). A transliteration records the consonants, such as *pr*; a translation gives the meaning, “go out” (Collier and Manley). Spoken forms used today add conventional vowels for convenience (Collier and Manley).

- **Independent checks.** Two observations that played no part in framing the hypothesis broke it. In December 1821 Champollion counted 1,419 hieroglyphic signs on the Rosetta Stone against 486 Greek words, far too many signs for one picture per word (Parkinson). Then, among copies from Abu Simbel received on 14 September 1822, a cartouche gave a native king’s name, Ramesses: a sun sign read as Ra, the sun, and final signs already known as *s* (Parkinson). Sound signs were not reserved for foreign names.

**What stays open.** A partial key can mislead: Young’s five correct values came with eight wrong ones (Parkinson). Vowels remain a modern convention.

The three hypotheses are the historical ones; the exercise layout and the one-row setting of the signs are the museum’s. **Verification status:** the sign values were checked in a second place: the mouth as *r* in Parkinson’s sign table and *pr* as “house” in Parkinson’s discussion of the House of Life; the sign numbers match their Unicode names, O001, D021 and D054. The meaning “go out” and the walking-legs determinative rest on Collier and Manley alone. Not reviewed by an Egyptologist.

#### Linear B: structure before sound

**Linear B is a script, not a cipher.** Nobody designed it to hide anything. Its signs record syllables and commodities in Mycenaean palace accounts, in an early form of Greek. When work began, both the sound values and the language were unknown, so there was no key to recover and no sender who wanted secrecy. Counting, grouping and testing predictions overlap with cryptanalysis; success meant a reading that independent evidence could check.

- **Structure first.** Alice Kober collected recurring words and showed that some appear in sets of three related forms, sharing a stem and changing their ending. She deliberately stopped before assigning sounds. From these relationships she drew up the beginning of a grid of signs that seemed to share a consonant or a vowel; Michael Ventris extended it.

- **The structure outlived a wrong interpretation.** Kober read her “triplets” as inflected forms of words. Ventris took a step she had not: he treated them as place names. Her first five proved to be the names of Cretan towns, with adjectives formed from them, and the relationships between signs that she had found still held.

- **The expected language had to give way.** Ventris’s working notes of 1951–52 tested Aegean and Etruscan parallels while Greek readings were still regarded as out of the question, and he favoured an Etruscan connection until mid-1952. He introduced his first full test of Greek, Work Note 20 of 1 June 1952, as a digression. Values from the grid then produced the names of Cretan towns and Greek words. The decipherment was announced on 1 July 1952.

- **The test came from a tablet not used to build the values.** Tablet Ta 641 was excavated at Pylos in 1952, and Carl Blegen studied it in spring 1953. Read with Ventris’s values, the words beside drawings of tripods and pots described those vessels: *ti-ri-po-de* beside tripods, *qe-to-ro-we* beside a four-handled pot, *a-no-we* beside one without handles. Blegen reported this in a letter of 16 May 1953. The tablet did not help form the values; it tested them.

- **Some readings stay open.** Linear B spelling leaves out *l*, *r* and *s* at the end of a syllable, and *m* and *n* before another consonant, so one written word can stand for different Greek words: *pa-te* can be *patēr*, “father”, or *pantes*, “all”. Context settles some cases: one tablet pairs *pa-te* with *ma-te*, “mother”, which points to *patēr*. Others remain open; even on Ta 641, Chadwick noted, the descriptions of the tripods allow several translations with no firm way to choose between them.

- **No confidence score.** Chadwick called the odds against Ta 641’s agreement arising by accident astronomical, and of a later check he wrote that such probabilities are “beyond mathematical analysis”. Ventris and Chadwick also pointed to the system’s success on texts found later. These are arguments, not measured probabilities, and this page does not turn them into a score.

**Original structural exercise:** the Latin labels below are invented, not Linear B signs, ancient words or a historical transcription. It isolates two steps: what repeated structure can and cannot establish, and how a held-out record tests an interpretation. It does not reproduce Kober’s grid or the decipherment.

Training groups — no meanings supplied; numbers are occurrences in an invented inventory

| Family | X form | Y form | Z form |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A | A X (2) | A Y (7) | A Z (1) |
| B | B X (1) | B Y (6) | B Z (1) |

Repeated X/Y/Z endings suggest a relationship. Y is by far the commonest ending, which makes it look like the basic form. Neither observation establishes which ending means one item, two items or something else, and neither supplies a sound.

Record an interpretation before checking it
- Y, the commonest ending, marks one item; X marks two

- X marks one item; Y marks two

Check against held-out recordsReset prediction

**Held-out exercise inventory:** one jar: C X; two jars: C Y; C Z beside a jar drawing with no numeral. These records were withheld from the training groups, as Ta 641 was absent from the evidence that produced Ventris’s values. They are part of this invented fixture.

Why was the commonest ending not the singular? An inventory mostly counts several items, so a plural can be its commonest form. Frequency is a clue about use, not a meaning.

#### Maya glyphs: letters, syllables and dates

**Maya writing is a script for Maya languages, not a code.** Mayan languages are living languages: the word read from the codex example below is still the word for turkey in modern Maya (Coe). The glyphs stayed unread because scholars assumed the wrong kind of system, not because anyone hid their meaning.

- **Source.** Bishop Diego de Landa’s account of Yucatán, written in Spain around 1566, survives in a later copy that the abbé Brasseur found in the nineteenth century. It sets Maya signs beside Spanish letters (Coe). The Maya codices add glyphs written beside pictures (Coe).

- **Transcription.** Landa recorded what his Maya informants wrote when he said each Spanish letter aloud. A letter such as b was spoken as a syllable, “be”, and the sign written in answer recorded that sound (Coe).

- **Structural hypothesis.** Taken at face value, the list is an alphabet: one sign for one letter. Readings built on that assumption failed, and Brasseur’s careless use of the list helped discredit phonetic approaches (Coe). When Knorozov later proposed phonetic readings, Eric Thompson attacked them (Coe).

- **Key.** Knorozov proposed in 1952 that most signs were syllables, a consonant plus a vowel, used alongside logograms that carry both meaning and sound. A word ending in a consonant is written with two syllables whose last vowel is silent, and that silent vowel usually echoes the first, a principle he called synharmony (Coe).

- **Reading.** Over a picture of a turkey in a codex stand Landa’s sign for *cu* and a second sign. Read as syllables, *k’u-tz(u)* gives *k’utz*, the word for turkey in Colonial and modern Maya (Coe). Later epigraphers refined the 1952 rule: an echoing silent vowel marks a short vowel, and a different one marks a long vowel, as in *ba-k(i)* for *baak* (Coe).

- **Independent checks.** The picture beside the glyphs and the living language both check the reading from outside the script (Coe). A separate check needed no phonetic reading at all. At Piedras Negras, Tatiana Proskouriakoff found that monuments fall into sets in which the earliest date, marked by a glyph she identified as birth, comes 12 to 31 years before a date marked by an accession glyph, and each set spans one ruler’s lifetime. The inscriptions record history, not only the calendar (Coe).

**What stays open.** Readings continue to be refined; the syllabic chart in Coe’s book records the values in use when the third edition appeared. The pronunciations used here are modern conventions.

#### Z340: replay the first 153 symbols

This is an authentic source-backed replay using the published key and the first nine rows of the November 1969 cipher. The grid uses case-sensitive ASCII symbol IDs from David Oranchak’s repository, not facsimiles of Zodiac’s handwriting. It retains all 63 symbol types.

Start at the upper left, move down one row and right two columns, wrapping independently at both edges of this 9 × 17 section. Compare that route with reading rows in order. The key stays fixed.

Known-key route replay

Route hypothesis
- Recovered route: down 1, right 2, wrapping

- Counterexample: ordinary row order
 Number of symbols read [Input route-step: type=range; initial value=0]

Read next symbolPlay routeComplete routeInspect in Evidence Workbench

Diplomatic reading: original spellings retained

Loading the published first-section fixture…

Published symbol-to-letter key

```text

```

**Do not repair the result invisibly.** The diplomatic output includes FAN, BRINGO, BECAASE and PARADLCE. A normalized interpretation reads FUN, BRINGS, BECAUSE and PARADISE. Those are editorial corrections, not different outputs from the key.

**This route does not solve all 340 symbols.** In the second nine-row section, the published reconstruction skips LIFE IS and corrects a shifted segment on row 15. The final two rows include reversed words. The eventual placement of LIFE IS is subjective. The physical procedure used to create the cipher remains uncertain.

The solvers submitted their report to the FBI on 5 December 2020; the public announcement and FBI confirmation followed on 11 December. The replay verifies a published solution mechanically. It does not reproduce the original search or turn a plausible sentence into independent historical proof.

Sources: [Oranchak, Blake and Van Eycke, “The Solution of the Zodiac Killer’s 340-Character Cipher”](<https://arxiv.org/abs/2403.17350>), §5.4–5.5, figures 43–44 and 53–54; [solver’s ciphertext and decoded-grid transcription](<https://github.com/doranchak/zodiac-killer-ciphers/blob/master/src/main/java/com/zodiackillerciphers/ciphers/Ciphers.java>). [Download the exact replay fixture and provenance](<https://ciphermuseum.com/data/research/v1/z340-section-1.json>).

#### What the comparison teaches

Copiale challenges the assumed role of a symbol. Mary Stuart challenges transcription, language and key structure. Z340 challenges reading order and makes exceptions visible. Linear B asks what recurring structure can establish before sounds are assigned. The Egyptian case asks which signs carry sound and which carry meaning, and the Maya case asks what kind of system a sign list really records. None of those three scripts was a cipher: each became readable once the right kind of system was proposed and then tested against evidence it had not been built from. Each case needs a different test. The workbench preserves those hypotheses and their evidence instead of treating readable output as a verdict.

[Build an evidence record](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html>) · [Use Cipher Detective](<https://ciphermuseum.com/cipher-detective.html>) · [Explore cryptanalysis techniques](<https://ciphermuseum.com/cryptanalysis.html>)

```json
    "scope": "Each script case's source note now states its verification status: what a second published source confirms, what rests on one source, and that no specialist has reviewed it. The Egyptian case links the Abydos king list (British Museum EA 117) on the Museum's collection site. The sign row uses a self-hosted three-glyph subset of Noto Sans Egyptian Hieroglyphs (SIL OFL 1.1).",
    "sources": "Second sources: Parkinson's sign table and House of Life discussion and Unicode character names (Egyptian); Kettunen and Helmke, Introduction to Maya Hieroglyphs, 20th edition (Wayeb, 2025), publisher-hosted PDF (Maya); Chadwick 1958 and Documents 1956 cross-checked (Linear B). The Thesaurus Linguae Aegyptiae page was behind a bot check and was not used.",
    "tests": "tests/test-case-verification.js failed before the change; eight planted defects (status wording, a claimed review, an unlinked second source, the British Museum link, a sign the font lacks, the sign row skipping the subset, the font missing from the offline cache, a case record still promising review) each failed it.",
    "limits": "No specialist review is available for any of the three cases."
  }
}
```

Research and evidence

### Evidence Workbench

Keep the original, compare readings, and make every decipherment hypothesis inspectable.

#### First visit? Start with three questions

You do not need to run a solver to assess an argument. Before importing a manuscript, try this short exercise inspired by chapter 7.

- A proposed reading works only after the reader changes several letter shapes. What was observed, and what was changed to fit the answer?

- The same reader chooses both the key and the passage used to test it. Which new passage could provide a separate test?

- Another reader cannot reproduce the result. What information would let them repeat the method exactly?

Compare your approach

Keep the original image and disputed transcriptions side by side. Write down the rule before testing it on a new passage. Record exceptions instead of silently adjusting the rule. A convincing sentence is a candidate; a reproducible method needs to explain more of the material.

For a guided fictional case with hints, [open the curator’s sealed letter](<https://ciphermuseum.com/sealed-letter.html>). The tools below are available when you want to investigate your own evidence.

Read a manuscript, preserve competing interpretations, and test a cipher hypothesis. Everything runs in this browser. Export your record before closing; source images stay on your device. [Start with the historical investigations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/decipherment-cases.html>) or [return to Cipher Detective](<https://ciphermuseum.com/cipher-detective.html>).

[Try four guided evidence exercises](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/reading-the-evidence.html>): match a key, separate cleartext from ciphertext, test transcription mistakes, and compare recurring symbols.

[Source](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#source>) · [Images](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#images>) · [Readings](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#annotations>) · [Symbol inventory](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#inventory>) · [Known keys](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#keys>) · [Number grouping](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#segmentation>) · [Homophonic search](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#homophonic>) · [Hypotheses](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#hypotheses>) · [Save and resume](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html#bundle>)

Start with a transcription or import a Cipher Detective JSON export.

Export evidence JSONRestore previous saved record

 Import version 1 or 2 transcription JSON (up to 750 KB) [Input import-file: type=file; initial value=]

#### Sounds right—but does it fit?

Two readable messages. One fixed cipher. Can both readings be right? In this invented exercise, each ciphertext symbol always stands for the same letter, different symbols stand for different letters, and there are no copying errors. Spaces in the candidates are supplied for readability.

**Recorded ciphertext:** `A B B C A B D C E F F E`

 Candidate reading
- SEND ME AT NOON

- MEET ME AT NOON

**Proposed plaintext:** SEND ME AT NOON

Pick a symbol to compare every position where it occurs. Count positions without spaces.

 Check the whole reading

The exercise loads when JavaScript is available.

What this check can—and cannot—tell you

For SEND ME AT NOON, symbol B would mean E at positions 2 and 6 but N at position 3. MEET ME AT NOON fits all the mappings. Consistency is necessary under this model; it does not establish authenticity or a unique solution. A noisy transcription or a different cipher needs a different test, with every exception recorded.

Related research: [Aldarrab and May, *Can Sequence-to-Sequence Models Crack Substitution Ciphers?* (2021)](<https://aclanthology.org/2021.acl-long.561/>). Their experiments study automatic decipherment, including noisy text. These two invented candidates are not outputs from that study; this exercise runs no neural model.

Next: [what if the original encipherer used the wrong key?](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/mary-stuart-castelnau-letters.html#key-confusion>)

#### 2. Register the source images

A record can carry several images. Each one keeps its **original pixel dimensions**, so a region recorded today still means the same part of the page after you zoom, rotate the view or crop it. Region coordinates are always stored against the original image and are never rewritten by a change of presentation.

Opening a file computes its **SHA-256 content hash**. That detects different bytes arriving under the same filename — a genuinely useful check. It is **not** an authenticity check: byte identity says two files are the same file, never that either is the archival object it claims to be. Keep the archive identifier and URL above for that.

#### 4. Build a revisable symbol inventory

Deciding which marks are the same symbol *is* research, not a step before it. Two strokes that were transcribed alike may be a letter and the same letter with a diacritic; two that look different may be one hand varying. Build the inventory, look at every occurrence of a class, and split or merge with a recorded reason before you apply any key.

Splitting moves **only the occurrences you select** and leaves the rest where they were. Merging keeps every earlier label and reason in the decision history. The whole history travels with the exported record.

How to divide the transcription

- Fixed-width groups

#### 5. Apply a known key

Historical nomenclators mix letters, syllables, whole words, names and controls. This tool applies an entered key; it does not recover one. Multiple readings stay unresolved. The explicit teaching convention for `repeat` and `erase` acts on one preceding resolved Unicode character; check the manuscript’s actual convention.

Typed key entries (JSON array)

[ {"symbol":"g1","type":"letter","values":["A"]}, {"symbol":"g2","type":"name","values":["MARY"]}, {"symbol":"g3","type":"space","values":[]}, {"symbol":"g4","type":"null","values":[]}, {"symbol":"g5","type":"word","values":["KING","QUEEN"]} ]

Linguistic types: letter, syllable, word, name, number, content-word, function-word, ending, phrase, sentence, punctuation. Operational types: null, space, repeat, erase; unknown preserves an unresolved reading. This example is invented, not Mary Stuart’s historical key.

Whitespace-separated symbol IDs

g1 g3 g2 g4 g3 g5

Apply entered keySave key in evidence recordLoad invented ending and punctuation example

Example: 21 → “seal” (word), 22 → “ed” (ending), 23 → “.” (punctuation); 21 22 23 emits “sealed.” These are linguistic content, not alphabet letters or null/repeat/erase instructions. [What Was Encoded in Historical Cipher Keys in the Early Modern Era?](<https://ecp.ep.liu.se/index.php/histocrypt/article/view/404>), pp. 2–8, motivates the taxonomy, not these invented entries. A code's century or frequency does not establish its meaning. The unknown-key search below remains letter-only.

#### 6. Compare numerical segmentations

A known key can leave the boundaries ambiguous: `2228` can be `2|2|2|8`, `2|22|8` or `22|2|8`. Compare readings without changing the original digits. This is bounded known-key path enumeration, not unknown-key cryptanalysis.

The search builds a **lattice** over the digit stream, discards positions that cannot reach the end, then either enumerates every complete path or — when there are more paths than the budget allows — keeps a bounded best-first set. The result says which happened, and whether the globally best path is still guaranteed.

Exact digit stream (1–80 digits; line breaks allowed)

2228

Code-to-reading key (JSON object)

{"2":"A","22":"N","8":"D"}

A plain string is one reading. A typed entry is `{"type":"null","values":[]}`, `{"type":"word","values":["AND","ALSO"]}` or `{"type":"name","values":["MARY"]}`. Types: letter, syllable, word, name, null.

Mandatory cuts after these digit positions (comma separated) [Input segment-locks: type=text; initial value=]

Search-node budget (1–20,000) [Input segment-budget: type=number; initial value=10000] Line breaks in the source
- Are mandatory cuts: no code may span one

- May be spanned by a code
Ranking model
- Neutral: preserve equal alternatives

- Museum modern-English n-gram model

At most 32 complete paths are retained. Scores compare candidates; they are not probabilities. The English model is unsuitable for historical French, Italian, German or Latin, and assigns zero to readings shorter than three letters.

 Compare segmentations

Where the boundaries are actually ambiguous

A position is **forced** when every complete path cuts there, **impossible** when none does, and genuinely ambiguous otherwise. This view is read from the lattice, so it is exact even when the retained paths are only a bounded sample.

#### 7. Search a homophonic letter key (experimental)

Several symbol IDs may map to the same letter. Two bounded solvers are offered, and they are offered *against* each other rather than as a recommendation. Short texts can still produce persuasive errors; inspect alternatives and use independent evidence. Neither handles nulls, word codes, control signs, uncertain symbols or transposition. Resolve those hypotheses first.

- **Simulated annealing** mutates a complete key under a cooling schedule, with periodic restarts. It is seeded: replay needs the same input, options, model/solver build and JavaScript engine. Cross-engine floating-point behavior can change the search path.

- **Beam search** fixes symbols one at a time in order of first appearance — a disclosed extension order chosen so every step lengthens the decoded prefix that gets scored. The partial score is informative but **not admissible**: pruning can discard the branch holding the correct key. It is deterministic and needs no seed.

**Annealing is the stronger of the two. Beam search is here as a contrast, not as a better option.** A pre-registered tuning sweep — four beam widths crossed with three scoring weights, against annealing at two budgets — found that **every annealing setting beat every beam setting**. The best beam configuration reached 76.7% mean plaintext error against the shipped annealing row’s 71.9%, while taking about 126 times as long. Choose beam search to see how a different search strategy behaves on the same input, not because it will do better.

The full sweep table, the measured head-to-head comparison on identical inputs and language resources, and what the result means are in [research-evaluation v2](<https://ciphermuseum.com/data/research/v2/README.md>). Neither solver recovered any held-out text there, and that section explains what that does and does not imply.

 Whitespace-separated symbol IDs (3–600 tokens; up to 100 distinct IDs)

Solver
- Simulated annealing (seeded)

- Beam search (deterministic; contrast only, weaker)
 Scoring model

The modern-English baseline is cached for offline use. The historical profiles are a 0.74 MB download fetched on demand and are deliberately **not** in the offline bundle, so selecting one needs a connection.

##### Compare language hypotheses

Paste or decode a candidate reading and compare it against the versioned historical-language profiles. The comparison ranks profiles by mean log-probability per letter and **declines to choose** when the input is too short, the margin too small or the profile’s coverage of this text too low. A profile names a language *and* a period. A language with no profile can never be chosen, only abstained on or mis-assigned.

 Candidate reading (letters A–Z; everything else is ignored)

Compare language profilesUse the top search candidate

What would count as evidence?

The receipt records the exact input, solver, locks, seed or beam width, budget, model, candidate keys and score trace. A high score alone does not establish a historical reading. Check the mapping against every occurrence, account for exceptions, and seek evidence that did not select the candidate. Short or damaged texts can yield convincing errors.

The public generated exercise reads THE SECRET LETTER IS IN THE CHEST… It is a practice fixture, not a blind benchmark. [Read the v1 evaluation protocol](<https://ciphermuseum.com/data/research/v1/README.md>) and [the v2 task matrix and measured results](<https://ciphermuseum.com/data/research/v2/README.md>).

#### 8. Keep a visible hypothesis register

A key that applies cleanly to every symbol is a mechanical result. It is not evidence of who wrote the letter, when, or what the words meant to its reader. The register keeps each claim beside the evidence for it, the evidence against it, and the alternatives nobody has ruled out.

Confidence is a **word from a fixed list**, not a number: `unsupported`, `possible`, `probable`, `well-supported`, `contradicted`. Calling a claim well-supported requires at least two separately recorded pieces of supporting evidence and no unanswered counterevidence — the record refuses it otherwise. Repeating the same reference does not count twice. Different reference identifiers are only a minimum check; assess whether the observations are actually independent.

Claim

 Confidence
- unsupported

- possible [selected]

- probable

- well-supported

- contradicted
 Unresolved alternatives, one per line

Supporting evidence: `identifier | what it shows`, one per line

 Contradictory evidence, same format

 Note

Record hypothesisLoad the unidentified-document case

#### 9. Save and resume the whole investigation

Exporting the evidence record alone loses everything around it: which derived layer you were reading, which mappings you had locked, which solver and scoring model produced a candidate, and which candidate you chose. A **versioned investigation bundle** carries all of it in one file, and importing one in a fresh session restores the declared state.

Two honesty constraints are built in. **Stale results are marked, never silently reused**: the bundle fingerprints its inputs, and a changed transcription, key or solver input shows up as stale on import. And **image bytes stay on your device** — the bundle carries a manifest of filenames, dimensions and hashes so you can reopen the right files yourself. Nothing is fetched from a URL on your behalf.

Replay also needs the same solver and scoring-model build, not only the same seed. The bundle records the build it was made with and tells you when they differ.

#### Research behind the workbench

- [Historical-cipher transcription conventions (2020)](<https://ep.liu.se/en/conference-article.aspx?Article_No=14&issue=171&series=ecp>): preserve the source and uncertainty.

- [Numerical cipher segmentation (2022)](<https://aclanthology.org/2022.emnlp-main.44/>): known keys can still leave multiple paths.

- [Lasry, Biermann and Tomokiyo (2023), Appendix A](<https://doi.org/10.1080/01611194.2022.2160677>): annealing helps with letter homophones; nomenclature and archival corroboration still require human work.

Collection · checking · interpretation

### From Intercept to Intelligence

A readable message is one link in the evidence chain.

#### 1 · Preserve the intercept

**Original fictional exercise.** This is a small teaching analogy for an Army/Air Force workflow: Hut 6 worked on Enigma cryptanalysis and Hut 3 on translation and intelligence assessment. It is not a wartime intercept, an Enigma simulation or a naval Hut 8/4 reconstruction.

A complete intelligence process involves collection, transcription, checking, indexing and interpretation. A machine’s candidate stop is not a finished intelligence report.

L1 — original traffic log

| Time (exercise clock) | Source | Destination | Observation |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 09:12 | R4 | Q2 | One message copied by receiver North, record I1 |
| 09:16 | Q2 | R4 | Short response recorded; content unavailable |

**I1 — receiver North:** `PHHW DW ??VW JDWH`. Two unreadable symbols remain as `??`.

For this exercise only, a known Caesar shift of +3 was supplied with the fixture. Subtract 3 to read it; this is not an attack on Enigma.

**Literal reading:** ``

#### 2 · Index without guessing away the gaps

**Index card C1:** an earlier exercise log associated callsign Q2 with deliveries near the east gate. This association may be stale; it supplies neither missing letters nor the identity of today’s operator.

Candidate completion
- WEST

- EAST

**Proposed reading:** ``

Compare independently logged copy I2Reset comparison

**I2 — receiver South, same 09:12 transmission:** `PHHW DW HDVW JDWH`. A separately logged complete copy is independent of our proposed completion, but it is still a copy of the same transmission, not a second order or proof of an event.

#### 3 · Trace the assessment to its evidence

Which claim would you report?
- Traffic was recorded between R4 and Q2

- The complete copy instructs a meeting at the east gate

- Identified officers met at the east gate
Check the evidence boundary

Your report: claim, source IDs, uncertainty and next check

Notes remain on this page only and disappear on reload. Continue in the [Evidence Workbench](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html>) to keep a revisable source record, or compare the [Bombe’s constraint checks](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/bombe.html>).

#### Historical reading

Gordon Welchman, *The Hut Six Story* (1984 edition), chapters 9–10, especially printed pp. 156–161, connects traffic handling and cryptanalytic organization. Stephen Budiansky, *Battle of Wits* (2002 edition), printed pp. 211–215, discusses the organization of information and indexing. These books inform the process questions; all callsigns, records, ciphertext and interface wording in this exercise are original.

[Bletchley Park](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/bletchley-park.html>) · [Other evidence cases](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/decipherment-cases.html>)

[Entrance](<https://ciphermuseum.com/index.html>) › [Lab](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/workbench.html>) › Paired Messages

Cryptanalytic method · original exerciseBletchley Park context

### One Message, Two Encryptions

A weak copy can supply the plaintext an analyst needs to attack another copy.

#### Why Codebreakers Welcomed a “Kiss”

At Bletchley Park, a message sent under different keys or through different cipher systems could be called a *kiss*. Reading one version could supply a crib for another. Bauer describes general orders repeated across Enigma networks and messages also carried by simpler systems.

The shared element is the message. This is different from the [Lorenz depth](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/lorenz.html>), where related messages reused a keystream. Here the copies may use different keys and algorithms.

**Everything below is invented.** Copy A uses Caesar with a shift of 4, assumed already known to the analyst. Copy B uses repeating-key Vigenère with a known period of five. Both are broken historical cipher families; Copy B is only harder within this teaching comparison. This is not an Enigma simulation.

#### Test Whether the Copies Share a Plaintext

Predict: if you recover five letters of Copy A, can they determine five Vigenère key letters? Would fitting those five positions prove that the other copy says the same thing?

Invented dispatch pair
- Same plaintext in two systems

- Control: different plaintexts, same length

**Copy A · Caesar**

**Copy B · Vigenère**

Spaces are removed and X is padding in these fixtures. No real intercepted message is reproduced.

##### 1. Read the weaker copy

Apply the already known Caesar shift. In a real case, obtaining that weak-system solution would be a separate task.

Decrypt Copy A with shift 4

##### 2. Fit a candidate key

Assume the first five letters of A also begin B. With A = 0, B = 1, …, Z = 25, subtract plaintext from ciphertext modulo 26. Only the first five positions are used here.

Derive five candidate key letters

Candidate key from the crib

| Position | B cipher | A plain | Key calculation |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

##### 3. Test the remaining positions

The first five positions fit by construction. Now decrypt B and compare positions 6–20 with the rest of A. Then select the different-message control and repeat the investigation.

Check the held-out positionsReset this pair

Enable JavaScript for the paired-message experiment. The historical explanation, assumptions and sources remain available without it.

#### What the Result Establishes

A successful check supports the shared-plaintext hypothesis within this controlled fixture. Equal lengths, a plausible phrase or a fitted key alone would not authenticate a historical message. The negative control gives you a case where a key fits the crib but fails elsewhere.

Known plaintext is an expected attacker capability for modern encryption. Sending the same plaintext under independent keys does not by itself break a sound modern scheme. The key recovery here works because repeating-key Vigenère exposes the repeated shifts when plaintext is known.

Continue with [Enigma’s crib checks](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/enigma.html>), [known-plaintext cryptanalysis](<https://ciphermuseum.com/cryptanalysis.html>) or [the Evidence Workbench](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/evidence-workbench.html>).

#### Sources and Reading

Friedrich L. Bauer, *Decrypted Secrets: Methods and Maxims of Cryptology*, 4th ed. (Springer, 2007), pp. 288–289 and 419–421, especially §19.5.1. The history is paraphrased; the messages, controls and exercise are original museum material.

Tony Sale’s [Virtual Bletchley Park account of registration and traffic analysis](<https://www.codesandciphers.org.uk/virtualbp/gwelchman/gwelch.htm>) explains the contemporary use of “kisses.” The exercise uses neither historical intercepts nor historical key material.
