[Entrance](<https://ciphermuseum.com/index.html>)› Learn

 Understanding Encryption

### How Ciphers Work

From pen-and-paper codes to the mathematics behind modern security

#### Start with one message

[Your first codebreak](<https://ciphermuseum.com/first-codebreak.html>) guides you through a library notice, repeating-key Vigenère, language assumptions and homophonic counts. Try the first lesson in about 15 minutes.

[Find a classical cipher topic](<https://ciphermuseum.com/classical-guide.html>) · [Print the activities](<https://ciphermuseum.com/codebreak-worksheet.html>)

Why History Matters

#### What Classical Ciphers Can Teach

Historical ciphers offer useful comparisons with modern substitution, permutation and key management. These comparisons explain concepts; they do not establish a direct historical path from a classical system to AES or another modern design.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-gear"></use></svg>
```

Substitution → S-Boxes

Classical substitutions illustrate a basic idea also present in AES. Its nonlinear S-boxes operate with key mixing and diffusion; a fixed substitution table alone does not provide modern security.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-globe"></use></svg>
```

Polyalphabetic → Stream Ciphers

Vigenère's repeating keyword and ChaCha20's pseudorandom keystream illustrate combining a message with a keystream. A ChaCha20 key can protect multiple messages, but nonces must not repeat under that key, and the block counter must not wrap.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-puzzle"></use></svg>
```

Transposition → Permutation

Classical transpositions permute symbols. AES ShiftRows permutes byte positions and MixColumns combines bytes linearly; these are useful conceptual comparisons, not a claim of direct historical descent.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-gear"></use></svg>
```

Rotor Machines → Block Ciphers

Enigma changes its substitution as it steps. Modern block ciphers use repeated designed round operations. Compare the mechanisms without treating one as a direct implementation of the other.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-diamond"></use></svg>
```

Lorenz → Computing

Manual cryptanalysis recovered Lorenz messages and inferred the machine before Colossus. From February 1944, Colossus accelerated statistical searches for wheel settings; it was an important early programmable electronic computer.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-puzzle"></use></svg>
```

One-Time Pad → Perfect Secrecy

Shannon formalized perfect secrecy: an OTP achieves it for fixed-length messages when the pad is uniformly random, secret, independent of the message and never reused. Practical encryption often targets computational security with shorter keys. Information-theoretic and computational security are different guarantees, with different assumptions.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="section-divider" viewBox="0 0 180 6"><line x1="0" y1="3" x2="180" y2="3"></line></svg>
```

#### Three details that change a protocol

Follow these worked examples, then continue in the linked Crypto Lab activities:

- [A valid tag versus a fresh message](<https://ciphermuseum.com/modern.html#replay-freshness>): replay A/B/A and see what receiver state changes.

- [Field boundaries before hashing](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/sha256.html#structured-hashing>): distinguish different records from different input bytes.

- [Separate keys after key agreement](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/diffie-hellman.html#key-derivation>): compare HKDF purpose labels and output lengths.

Continue Learning

#### Explore by Topic

[Deep Dive

##### Modern Cryptography

Compare classical cipher mechanisms with AES, RSA and post-quantum security, and learn the assumptions behind computational security.

Advanced

→](<https://ciphermuseum.com/modern.html>) [Techniques

##### Cryptanalysis Techniques

Frequency analysis, Kasiski examination, and index of coincidence — tools that break many classical ciphers given suitable ciphertext, rather than every historical system or a correctly used one-time pad.

Codebreaking

→](<https://ciphermuseum.com/cryptanalysis.html>) [Reference

##### Glossary

Definitions of selected cryptographic terms used in the museum — from plaintext to public keys.

Reference

→](<https://ciphermuseum.com/glossary.html>) [Compare

##### Cipher Comparison

Side-by-side comparison of a selection of cipher systems and context exhibits — type, era, security status, and key properties.

Analysis

→](<https://ciphermuseum.com/comparison.html>) [Interactive

##### Cipher Detective

Paste unknown ciphertext and get evidence-based candidate families from IoC, Kasiski, chi-square, and character-set analysis.

Analysis Lab

→](<https://ciphermuseum.com/cipher-detective.html>) [Hall XII

##### Unsolved Ciphers

Study open problems like Voynich, Kryptos K4, and Somerton Man with current research framing and evidence-first methodology.

Research

→](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/unsolved.html>) [Hall XIII

##### Cipher Culture

Explore how ciphers move into novels, television, ARGs, and puzzle hunts while preserving real cryptanalytic ideas.

Culture

→](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/culture.html>)

New to Cryptography?

#### Start with the Caesar Cipher

A fixed alphabet shift is a useful place to begin. Explore the cipher associated with Julius Caesar, then compare its limitations with the requirements of modern encryption.

 [Start with Caesar →](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/caesar.html>)

#### Reading

Joshua Holden, *The Mathematics of Secrets: Cryptography from Caesar Ciphers to Digital Encryption* (2017 edition), chapters 4–5; Claude E. Shannon, “Communication Theory of Secrecy Systems” (1949), §§10 and 23. These support the conceptual comparisons and perfect-secrecy conditions.

[Entrance](<https://ciphermuseum.com/index.html>)›Modern Cryptography

 Beyond the Museum

### Modern Cryptography

Not just ciphers — an entirely different discipline

This collection includes ciphers, people, historical and cultural context, mathematical foundations, hashes, signatures, and protocols. A cipher reversibly transforms plaintext into ciphertext with a key, but many exhibits explain other parts of cryptography. This page connects that broader history to modern practice.

The Complete Journey

#### Classical → Modern: Compare Historical Weaknesses with Modern Design

| Classical Cipher Type | Historical Limitation | Modern Solution | Modern Example |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Caesar / Monoalphabetic | Frequency analysis — letter mapping preserved | Nonlinear substitution combined with diffusion and key mixing | AES SubBytes |
| Homophonic Substitution | One-to-many substitution can retain language and structural clues | Computational security under a defined attack model | AES in an appropriate authenticated mode |
| Polyalphabetic / Vigenère | Repeating key creates detectable periodicity | Pseudorandom keystreams with per-key nonce uniqueness and counter limits | ChaCha20, AES-GCM |
| Transposition (Rail Fence, Columnar) | Letter counts preserved; language and ordering clues can help analysis | Nonlinear substitution combined with diffusion and key mixing | AES ShiftRows + MixColumns |
| Playfair / Hill (block) | Small blocks leak digraph statistics; linear algebra solvable | 128-bit blocks, non-linear operations, round keys | AES (128-bit block) |
| Fractionation (Bifid, ADFGVX) | Coordinate mixing insufficient with static key square | Multiple rounds of mixing with key-derived round keys | AES 10–14 rounds |
| Layered classical systems | Substitution + transposition — each layer still attackable | Multiple designed rounds; security depends on the whole construction | AES, Camellia, SM4 |
| Machine ciphers (Enigma, Lorenz) | Physical key distribution; operator errors; structural flaws | Authenticated public-key methods help establish shared secrets | RSA, Diffie-Hellman, ECDH |
| One-Time Pad | Impractical key management — reuse is catastrophic | Computationally secure with short key; KDFs for key derivation | AES-256, X25519 key exchange |

ExampleChaCha20, AES-GCM

SolutionNonlinear substitution combined with diffusion and key mixing

ExampleAES ShiftRows + MixColumns

Playfair / Hill (block)

Solution128-bit blocks, non-linear operations, round keys

ExampleAES (128-bit block)

Fractionation (Bifid, ADFGVX)

WeaknessCoordinate mixing insufficient with static key square

SolutionMultiple rounds of mixing with key-derived round keys

ExampleAES 10–14 rounds

Layered classical systems

SolutionMultiple designed rounds; security depends on the whole construction

ExampleAES, Camellia, SM4

SolutionAuthenticated public-key methods help establish shared secrets

ExampleRSA, Diffie-Hellman, ECDH

One-Time Pad

ExampleAES-256, X25519 key exchange

Shannon's Framework

#### Confusion & Diffusion

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-puzzle"></use></svg>
```

Confusion

Making the relationship between key and ciphertext as complex as possible. Caesar has a simple key relationship: C = P + 3. One known pair reveals the entire key.

**Modern solution:** AES S-boxes are highly non-linear. The S-box has a precise public algebraic definition. Its nonlinearity works with the other round operations; an S-box alone does not make encryption secure.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-globe"></use></svg>
```

Diffusion

Spreading each plaintext bit's influence across many ciphertext bits. Caesar does not spread changes across letter positions: change one letter, change exactly one ciphertext letter.

**Modern solution:** AES combines ShiftRows and MixColumns to spread byte dependencies. Across suitable inputs, changing one bit of a full AES input changes about half the output bits on average; this is not an exact per-message promise or a standalone security proof.

The Modern Landscape

#### What Replaced Classical Cryptography

⚠️

**Taxonomy note:** Symmetric and public-key encryption schemes both reversibly transform plaintext into ciphertext. RSA encryption and RSA signatures are distinct schemes with different encoding and padding requirements. Diffie–Hellman establishes shared secrets; signatures authenticate messages; hash functions produce digests. These primitives solve different problems and must be combined with appropriate protocols.

🏛️

**Looking for the exhibits?** Explore these constructions — [DES](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/des.html>), [Diffie-Hellman](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/diffie-hellman.html>), [RSA](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/rsa.html>), [AES](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/aes.html>), and [SHA-256](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/sha256.html>) — and further exhibits in [Hall XI · Modern Cryptography](<https://ciphermuseum.com/halls/modern-crypto.html>). This page provides the wider context: the journey from classical failure to modern practice, and additional constructions such as ChaCha20, ECDH and post-quantum schemes.

🔐 Ciphers — Plaintext → Ciphertext → Plaintext

[[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-diamond"></use></svg>
```

AES-256 (Symmetric) → exhibit

128-bit blocks, 256-bit key, 14 rounds. 2²⁵⁶ possible keys; no practical key-recovery attack is known against correctly used full-round AES. Protocol, mode and implementation failures remain separate risks.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/aes.html>)

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-globe"></use></svg>
```

ChaCha20 (Stream Cipher)

Designed by Daniel Bernstein. Original ChaCha uses a 64-bit nonce and 64-bit block counter. IETF ChaCha20 (RFC 8439) uses a 256-bit key, 96-bit nonce, and 32-bit block counter. Both XOR plaintext with a deterministic pseudorandom keystream: never reuse a nonce under the same key, and never let the block counter wrap. [Original specification](<https://cr.yp.to/chacha/chacha-20080128.pdf>); [RFC 8439 §§2.3, 4](<https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc8439.html#section-2.3>) and [errata](<https://www.rfc-editor.org/errata/rfc8439>).

🔑 Key Exchange — Creating a Shared Secret

[[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-person"></use></svg>
```

Diffie-Hellman Key Exchange → exhibit

Derives a shared secret over a public channel. Computing the shared group element is the computational Diffie–Hellman problem. Discrete logarithms provide one route to solving it; protocol security also needs authentication. *Not a cipher — it does not encrypt data.*](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/diffie-hellman.html>)

🔓 Public-Key Cryptography — Asymmetric Encryption & Signatures

[[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-key"></use></svg>
```

RSA (Asymmetric) → exhibit

Rivest, Shamir, Adleman (1977). Two keys — public (encrypt) and private (decrypt) — based on arithmetic modulo a product of large primes; factoring that composite reveals the private key. Used in TLS, email, and digital signatures.](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/rsa.html>)

#️⃣ Hash Functions — One-Way Fingerprints

[[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-lock"></use></svg>
```

SHA-256 (Hash Function) → exhibit

Produces a fixed 256-bit digest of a message shorter than 2⁶⁴ bits. Preimage resistance means finding an input for a chosen digest should be computationally difficult; low-entropy inputs can still be guessed. Generic collision search takes roughly 2¹²⁸ work, rather than having a proven exact lower bound. Used in digital signatures, certificate verification, blockchain. *A hash is not a reversible encryption scheme; guessed inputs can still be tested.*](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/sha256.html>)

🔮 The Future — Post-Quantum Cryptography

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-flask"></use></svg>
```

Post-Quantum Cryptography

Sufficiently large, fault-tolerant quantum computers could break conventional RSA and Diffie–Hellman using Shor’s algorithm. NIST standardized the first three algorithms from its post-quantum project in 2024: **ML-KEM** (formerly CRYSTALS-Kyber, key encapsulation) and **ML-DSA** (formerly CRYSTALS-Dilithium, signatures), both based on structured lattice problems. NIST also standardized the hash-based SLH-DSA signature scheme in 2024. These are intended to resist known quantum attacks; they are not proofs against every future attack. The mathematics is on exhibit in the Hall of Foundations: [lattices & the SVP](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/lattices-svp.html>), [the closest-vector problem](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/closest-vector.html>), [Learning With Errors](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/learning-with-errors.html>) — an introduction to the mathematical setting of lattice-based standards — and [polynomial rings](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/polynomial-rings.html>).

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-quill"></use></svg>
```

Elliptic-Curve Signatures → exhibit

[ECDSA](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/ecdsa.html>) signatures verify under a public key rather than concealing content; attribution to a person requires a trusted key association: a private scalar on an elliptic curve, a public point, and a signature anyone can check. Suitable 256-bit elliptic-curve groups and 3072-bit RSA are commonly compared at roughly 128 bits of classical security, under their respective assumptions. Security requires an appropriate curve and implementation. Reused or biased nonces, side-channel leakage, faults and validation errors can compromise a signer. Shor’s algorithm also applies to the elliptic-curve discrete logarithm problem. The group law is in [the Hall of Foundations](<https://ciphermuseum.com/hall-of-foundations/elliptic-curves-real.html>).

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-puzzle"></use></svg>
```

Threshold Secret Sharing → exhibit

[Shamir’s Secret Sharing](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/shamir-secret-sharing.html>) (1979) represents the secret in a finite field and samples the remaining polynomial coefficients independently and uniformly. With distinct nonzero share coordinates, k valid shares reconstruct the secret; fewer than k preserve its prior distribution. This information-theoretic property also holds against quantum computation, but does not protect stolen shares or faulty implementations. Threshold signing protocols can build on secret-sharing ideas but require additional protections; plain Shamir sharing does not authenticate shares or perform signatures.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-mask"></use></svg>
```

Zero-Knowledge Proofs → exhibit

[Zero-knowledge proofs](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/zero-knowledge-proofs.html>) combine a soundness guarantee with a specified bound on what the verifier can learn beyond the statement. The guarantee depends on the protocol and security model; toy protocols can have a visible nonzero soundness error. The succinct variants — zk-SNARKs and STARKs — validate private transactions and compress whole blocks of computation into a proof small enough to check on a blockchain. The Fiat–Shamir transformation connects certain interactive identification protocols to noninteractive proofs or signatures under additional assumptions. A signature is not automatically a general-purpose zero-knowledge proof.

#### A valid tag can belong to an old message

Suppose A carries counter 1 and B carries counter 2. Each tag covers the counter and message under the same session key. An observer records both. Compare a receiver that remembers only the last token with one that accepts a valid tag only when its counter exceeds the highest accepted counter, initially 0.

Swipe or scroll the table horizontally to see every column. Keyboard: focus the table area and use the arrow keys.

Deliver A, B, then A again; authenticate before updating state

| Delivery | Tag check | Last-token-only rule | Increasing-counter rule |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A (1) | Valid | Accept | Accept; highest = 1 |
| B (2) | Valid | Accept | Accept; highest = 2 |
| A (1), replayed | Valid | Accept | Reject; highest = 2 |
| A changed to counter 3; old tag | Invalid | Reject | Reject; highest = 2 |
| Forget receiver state, keep key; replay A (1) | Valid | Accept | Accept; highest = 1 |

The A/B/A sequence defeats last-token-only checking. Resetting state under the unchanged key defeats the counter rule too. Rejected packets must not advance the accepted counter. This simplified rule rejects out-of-order messages; transports that allow reordering need a defined replay window and session lifecycle, including counter exhaustion. Replaying recorded ciphertext does not require encrypting a new message with a reused nonce: sender nonce uniqueness and receiver freshness are separate requirements.

Try the existing [Crypto Lab: Regex Veil](<https://systemslibrarian.github.io/crypto-lab-fte/>), in its **Freshness** section: open authenticated fragments twice, then reset the used-counter window. It is a local protocol teaching model, not a deployed transport.

Sources: Ross Anderson, *Security Engineering: A Guide to Building Dependable Distributed Systems*, third edition (2020), §4.3, printed pp. 122–124 (PDF pp. 169–171); David Wong, *Real-World Cryptography* (2021 edition), §3.3.3, PDF pp. 140–143 in the reflowed edition, which has no printed pagination. The table is a museum teaching example.

🎓

**The museum's final lesson:** Many historical systems failed through structural weaknesses, key reuse, or operational mistakes; others are obsolete or unresolved. A correctly used one-time pad retains perfect secrecy. Modern cryptography adds computational security and public-key methods, while still depending on sound key management, and extends into protocols, signatures, and proofs.

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-book"></use></svg>
```

The Code Book

Simon Singh’s *The Code Book* traces the history of cryptography from ancient Egypt to quantum computing. A popular historical introduction, first published in 1999.

[Inline SVG: original diagram/icon markup]

```html
<svg class="icon" width="17" height="17"><use href="images/icons.svg#i-signal"></use></svg>
```

Cryptography I (Stanford)

Dan Boneh’s Coursera course covers modern cryptographic primitives: stream ciphers, block ciphers, MACs, public-key encryption, and key exchange.

#### Research case studies: what was actually attacked?

[UA-8295: altered firmware and forensic evidence](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/ua-8295.html>) · [SoDark: eight-round radio linking protection](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/sodark.html>) · [NFC: authentication, configuration and counterfeit hardware](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/nfc-deployment.html>)

[Compare GPU search, GPRS faults and conditional-linear DES](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/modern-attack-evidence.html>). Each result identifies the target, attacker access and limits of the evidence. New comparisons cover [hardware prefetch leakage](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/modern-attack-evidence.html#dmp>), [historical AES cache/EM evidence](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/modern-attack-evidence.html#aes-historical-evidence>) and [conditional quantum resource estimates](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/modern-attack-evidence.html#quantum-resources>).

 Your Guide to the Collection

### Museum Map

13 halls, the Foundations Annex, 173 exhibits — one continuous story: how humanity learned to keep secrets

🧭

**Suggested route:** Start in Hall I and follow the historical connections, then visit the Foundations Annex for the mathematics. The halls cover overlapping traditions and different security goals, rather than one inevitable path from simple ciphers to a single “unbreakable” system.

Reading the Exhibits

#### Trust Labels

Cipher and protocol exhibits carry one of four labels indicating their modern security status. People, places, institutions, principles, fiction, cultural exhibits, and mathematical foundations carry no cipher trust label. Click a label on any exhibit to return here.

Broken

Cryptographically broken — historically important but unsafe to use today.

Historical Only

Obsolete or historical system; notes distinguish cryptanalysis from capture or other operational compromise.

Educational

A didactic construction for learning cryptographic concepts, not for security.

Secure (Modern)

Still considered secure when used correctly with modern key handling.

Architectural Floor Plan

Click any hall or exhibit to enter. Your last-visited hall is highlighted.

```html
        <line x1="1" y1="16" x2="31" y2="16" stroke="var(--gold-dim)" stroke-width=".5"></line>
        <polygon points="16,2 14,9 18,9" fill="var(--gold-dim)"></polygon>
        <text x="16" y="8" text-anchor="middle" fill="var(--gold-dim)" font-size="5" font-family="var(--fm)">N</text>
      </svg>
```

#### Research case studies: what was actually attacked?

[UA-8295: altered firmware and forensic evidence](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/ua-8295.html>) · [SoDark: eight-round radio linking protection](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/sodark.html>) · [NFC: authentication, configuration and counterfeit hardware](<https://ciphermuseum.com/ciphers/nfc-deployment.html>)

[Compare GPU search, GPRS faults and conditional-linear DES](<https://ciphermuseum.com/lab/modern-attack-evidence.html>). Each result identifies the target, attacker access and limits of the evidence.

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

'use strict';
(function(){
  /* "You are here" — reads localStorage for last visited hall */
  var KEY = 'ciphermuseum.lastHall';
  var hallId = '';
  try { hallId = localStorage.getItem(KEY) || ''; } catch(e){}
  if(hallId){
    var room = document.querySelector('[data-hall-id="'+hallId+'"]');
    if(room){
      room.classList.add('bldg-here');
      var label = document.getElementById('youAreHereLabel');
      var wrap = document.getElementById('youAreHere');
      if(label && wrap){
        var name = room.querySelector('.bldg-room-name');
        label.textContent = 'Last visited: ' + (name ? name.textContent : hallId);
        wrap.style.display = '';
      }
      room.scrollIntoView({behavior:'smooth',block:'center'});
    }
  }
  /* Record hall visits from referrer */
  var ref = document.referrer;
  if(ref){
    var MAP = {ancient:'ancient',substitution:'substitution',polyalphabetic:'polyalphabetic',
      transposition:'transposition',military:'military','civil-war':'civil-war',
      machines:'machines',puzzle:'puzzle',unbreakable:'unbreakable',codebreakers:'codebreakers',
      'modern-crypto':'modern-crypto',unsolved:'unsolved',culture:'culture'};
    Object.keys(MAP).forEach(function(k){
      if(ref.indexOf('/halls/'+k+'.html')!==-1){
        try{ localStorage.setItem(KEY, MAP[k]); }catch(e){}
      }
    });
    if(ref.indexOf('/cryptanalysis.html')!==-1){
      try{ localStorage.setItem(KEY, 'lab'); }catch(e){}
    }
    if(ref.indexOf('/modern.html')!==-1){
      try{ localStorage.setItem(KEY, 'modern'); }catch(e){}
    }
  }
})();
```

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

(function(){
  var HALLS = [
    {id:'ancient',      label:'Hall I',   name:'World Origins of Cryptography', href:'halls/ancient.html', icon:'𓀂'},
    {id:'substitution', label:'Hall II',  name:'Classical Substitution', href:'halls/substitution.html', icon:'🔤'},
    {id:'polyalphabetic',label:'Hall III',name:'Polyalphabetic',         href:'halls/polyalphabetic.html', icon:'🔑'},
    {id:'transposition',label:'Hall IV',  name:'Transposition',          href:'halls/transposition.html', icon:'⟺'},
    {id:'military',     label:'Hall V',   name:'Military & Spy',         href:'halls/military.html', icon:'🎖'},
    {id:'civil-war',    label:'Hall VI',       name:'Civil War Gallery',      href:'halls/civil-war.html', icon:'⚔'},
    {id:'machines',     label:'Hall VII', name:'Mechanical Machines',     href:'halls/machines.html', icon:'⚙'},
    {id:'puzzle',       label:'Hall VIII', name:'Puzzle Ciphers',          href:'halls/puzzle.html', icon:'🧩'},
    {id:'unbreakable',  label:'Hall IX',    name:'Unbreakable Ciphers',    href:'halls/unbreakable.html', icon:'🔒'},
    {id:'codebreakers', label:'Hall X',       name:'Hall of Codebreakers',   href:'halls/codebreakers.html', icon:'🏛'},
    {id:'foundations',  label:'Annex',    name:'Hall of Foundations',    href:'hall-of-foundations/index.html', icon:'𝔽'}
  ];

  var resetInThisPage = false;
  function getPassed(){
    if(resetInThisPage) return [];
    try {
      var saved = JSON.parse(localStorage.getItem('ciphermuseum.path.passed'));
      if(!Array.isArray(saved)) return [];
      return saved.filter(function(id, i){
        return saved.indexOf(id) === i && HALLS.some(function(hall){ return hall.id === id; });
      });
    } catch(e){ return []; }
  }

  function render(){
    var passed = getPassed();
    var total = HALLS.length;
    var done = passed.length;

    /* Summary */
    document.getElementById('summary').innerHTML =
      '<div class="summary-stat"><span class="stat-num">' + done + ' / ' + total + '</span><span class="stat-label">Halls Completed</span></div>' +
      '<div class="summary-stat"><span class="stat-num">' + Math.round((done/total)*100) + '%</span><span class="stat-label">Progress</span></div>' +
      '<div class="summary-stat"><span class="stat-num">' + done + '</span><span class="stat-label">Badges Earned</span></div>';

    /* Grid */
    var grid = document.getElementById('pathGrid');
    grid.innerHTML = '';
    HALLS.forEach(function(h){
      var isPassed = passed.indexOf(h.id) !== -1;
      var card = document.createElement('div');
      card.className = 'path-card' + (isPassed ? ' passed' : '');

      var badgeColor = isPassed ? 'var(--gold)' : 'var(--s5)';
      var fillColor = isPassed ? 'var(--gold-glow)' : 'none';
      card.innerHTML =
        '<svg class="badge-svg" width="72" height="72" viewBox="0 0 80 80">' +
          '<circle cx="40" cy="40" r="36" fill="none" stroke="' + badgeColor + '" stroke-width="2"/>' +
          '<circle cx="40" cy="40" r="28" fill="' + fillColor + '" stroke="' + (isPassed?'var(--gold-dim)':'var(--s5)') + '" stroke-width="1"/>' +
          '<text x="40" y="44" text-anchor="middle" font-size="22">' + h.icon + '</text>' +
        '</svg>' +
        '<div class="card-hall">' + h.label + '</div>' +
        '<div class="card-name">' + h.name + '</div>' +
        '<div class="card-status ' + (isPassed?'done':'pending') + '">' + (isPassed ? '✓ Badge Earned' : '○ Not Yet Completed') + '</div>' +
        '<a class="card-link" href="' + h.href + '">' + (isPassed ? 'Revisit Hall' : 'Visit Hall') + '</a>';
      grid.appendChild(card);
    });
  }

  document.getElementById('resetBtn').addEventListener('click', function(){
    if(confirm('Reset all learning path progress? This cannot be undone.')){
      resetInThisPage = true;
      try {
        localStorage.removeItem('ciphermuseum.path.passed');
        localStorage.removeItem('ciphermuseum.path.active');
      } catch(e) { /* Storage unavailable: reset this page's displayed state. */ }
      render();
    }
  });

  render();
})();
```

### Popular Culture Survey

A filterable catalogue of how classical ciphers appear in stories, games, film, and internet puzzle communities — 14 entries across literature, screen media, music, art and puzzle traditions.

Periods identify a work’s publication or a tradition’s selected historical example, not its invention date. Follow each exhibit for sources and qualifications.

#### Embedded script 1 (JavaScript)

Source text, not a record of every rendered state. Any ebook URL is replaced by a stable omission ID; redacted code is for inspection, not execution.

```javascript

'use strict';
(function(){
  var DATA = [
    { title:'Poe, The Gold-Bug', medium:'literature', family:'substitution', period:'1843-1903', href:'ciphers/gold-bug.html', note:'Poe’s 1843 short story presents substitution decipherment through Legrand’s explanation of counts, patterns and likely words.' },
    { title:'Conan Doyle, Dancing Men', medium:'literature', family:'substitution', period:'1843-1903', href:'ciphers/dancing-men.html', note:'A cryptographic plot solved by pattern frequency and word shape — Holmes decodes by recognising E and common short words.' },
    { title:'Doyle, The Gloria Scott', medium:'literature', family:'concealment', period:'1843-1903', href:'ciphers/null-cipher.html', note:'Holmes breaks a null cipher hidden in a letter — every third word carries the real message, illustrated in the Null Cipher exhibit.' },
    { title:'The Da Vinci Code', medium:'literature', family:'multi-stage', period:'2000s', href:'ciphers/da-vinci-code.html', note:'Layered clue progression using substitutions and symbolic puzzles; the exhibit distinguishes fictional effects from the underlying cipher techniques.' },
    { title:'National Treasure', medium:'film', family:'multi-stage', period:'2000s', href:'ciphers/national-treasure.html', note:'Adventure-film chain of historical clue extraction and decoding; cipher literacy presented as heroic problem-solving.' },
    { title:'Gravity Falls Credits Ciphers', medium:'television', family:'substitution', period:'2010s', href:'ciphers/gravity-falls.html', note:'Broadcast audience puzzle loop built on Caesar/Atbash/A1Z26 variants; viewers can try the clues themselves.' },
    { title:'Cicada 3301', medium:'internet', family:'multi-stage', period:'2010s', href:'ciphers/cicada-3301.html', note:'Internet-scale collaborative cryptography and steganography hunt spanning three years and multiple continents.' },
    { title:'MIT Mystery Hunt', medium:'puzzle hunt', family:'multi-stage', period:'1980s', href:'ciphers/mit-mystery-hunt.html', note:'Annual team puzzle ecosystem featuring recurring cipher families; over 40 years of collaborative code-solving tradition.' },
    { title:'ARG puzzle practices', medium:'internet', family:'multi-stage', period:'multiple periods', href:'ciphers/krypto-arg.html', note:'ARG-era integration of text ciphers and media-layer clues; community collaborative decryption as social event.' },
    { title:'The BACH Motif', medium:'music', family:'substitution', period:'before 1800', href:'ciphers/bach-motif.html', note:'B-A-C-H uses German note names as a musical signature; the exhibit identifies its appearance in The Art of Fugue.' },
    { title:'Kryptos (Sanborn sculpture)', medium:'art', family:'multi-stage', period:'1990s', href:'ciphers/kryptos.html', note:'CIA headquarters sculpture bearing an 869-character ciphertext (865 letters plus four question marks) — K1–K3 publicly solved; K4 plaintext recovered from archival papers in 2025, distinct from a public cryptanalytic solution.' },
    { title:'Zodiac Killer Ciphers', medium:'historical document', family:'multi-stage', period:'1960s', href:'ciphers/zodiac.html', note:'Z408 solved in 1969; Z340 finally cracked in 2020 by an international team using candidate transpositions and homophonic-substitution search with language scoring.' },
    { title:'Sator Square in Tenet (2020)', medium:'film', family:'wordplay', period:'2010s', href:'ciphers/sator-square.html', note:'Ancient Latin word square revived in pop culture — notably in the film Tenet — as a symbol of palindromic mystery.' },
    { title:'Freemason Pigpen Tradition', medium:'tradition', family:'substitution', period:'before 1800', href:'ciphers/freemason-pigpen.html', note:'Grid-based substitution associated with Masonic manuscripts; the exhibit separates the historical tradition from a modern teaching alphabet.' }
  ];

  var resultWrap = document.getElementById('survey-results');
  var empty = document.getElementById('survey-empty');
  var m = document.getElementById('filter-medium');
  var f = document.getElementById('filter-family');
  var p = document.getElementById('filter-period');

  function render(){
    var mm = m.value, ff = f.value, pp = p.value;
    var rows = DATA.filter(function(item){
      return (mm === 'all' || item.medium === mm)
        && (ff === 'all' || item.family === ff)
        && (pp === 'all' || item.period === pp);
    });

    resultWrap.innerHTML = '';
    if(!rows.length){ empty.style.display = ''; return; }
    empty.style.display = 'none';

    rows.forEach(function(item){
      var card = document.createElement('article');
      card.className = 'survey-card';
      card.innerHTML =
        '<div class="survey-meta">' + item.medium + ' · ' + (item.period === '2010s' ? '2010s+' : item.period) + '</div>' +
        '<h3>' + item.title + '</h3>' +
        '<p style="margin:0 0 .75rem;line-height:1.65;color:var(--tx2);">' + item.note + '</p>' +
        '<a href="' + item.href + '" class="view-all-link">Open exhibit →</a>';
      resultWrap.appendChild(card);
    });
  }

  m.addEventListener('change', render);
  f.addEventListener('change', render);
  p.addEventListener('change', render);
  render();
})();
```

This report inventories the published v0.6 archive. The [corrected v0.8 dataset](<https://ciphermuseum.com/cipher-corpus.html#corrected-corpus>) has separate versioned statistics and verification notes.

#### Evaluation Status

**No solver accuracy has been measured for this report.** Earlier human, automated-solver and LLM percentages were unsupported estimates, not benchmark results, and have been removed. Difficulty labels are curriculum metadata, not calibrated success probabilities.

The downloadable “blind” split is public and includes answers. It is not confidential held-out data. Records labeled historical include teaching adaptations; the legacy verified flag does not establish source authenticity. See [dataset scope and errata](<https://ciphermuseum.com/public/corpus/README.md>).
